user: tell me how to prepare adequetly for cs 252

model: To prepare adequately for CS 25200 — Systems Programming, you should focus on the following areas:

1. **Review Prerequisites**: Make sure you have a good understanding of the content covered in CS 24000 (Programming in C) and CS 25100 (Data Structures and Algorithms). These courses lay the foundation for what you will learn in CS 25200.

2. **C Programming Skills**: Since CS 25200 emphasizes systems programming, proficiency in C is crucial. Brush up on C programming concepts such as pointers, memory management, and file I/O.

3. **Understanding Operating Systems**: Familiarize yourself with basic concepts of operating systems, as they often play a significant role in systems programming. Concepts like processes, threads, and memory management are important.

4. **Practice Coding**: Work on practical coding problems related to systems programming. Implementing data structures and algorithms in C can be particularly beneficial.

5. **Study Tools and Techniques**: Learn about debugging and development tools commonly used in systems programming, such as GDB (GNU Debugger).

6. **Engage with Course Resources**: Utilize any available resources such as textbooks, online forums, and study groups. Engage with your instructors and teaching assistants for additional guidance.

7. **Hands-on Projects**: If possible, work on small projects that require systems-level programming. This can include writing basic system utilities or small applications that interact with system APIs.

8. **Time Management**: Schedule regular study sessions leading up to the course to ensure that you are consistently reviewing the material and practicing your skills.

By focusing on these areas, you'll be in a strong position to succeed in CS 25200.

user: tell me about Purdue's gpu resources

==============================
current question: tell me about Purdue's gpu resources

model: Purdue University offers several GPU resources particularly optimized for machine learning and high-performance computing:

1. **Gilbreth Community Cluster**: This resource is specifically designed for GPU-intensive applications, consisting of Dell compute nodes equipped with Nvidia Tesla GPUs. Configuration includes various setups with different GPU types, such as V100, A100, A10, and A30. The nodes are interconnected via a 100 Gbps Infiniband network, enhancing data transfer speeds for computational tasks. Users can gain access through the Cluster Access Purchase page and subscribe to mailing lists for updates.

2. **Hammer**: Although primarily aimed at high-throughput computing, Hammer includes nodes with GPU capabilities and is suitable for users requiring extensive computational resources. It operates on a managed system with regular updates to maintain high performance.

3. **Access and Software**: Users interested in these GPU resources must request accounts, load specific software modules, and can access popular applications tailored for GPU computing. The system utilizes Slurm for job management, facilitating user operations through commands that ensure optimal resource utilization.

For detailed information, users can refer to the respective user guides and request portals provided by Purdue's Research Computing services.

current question: Tell me all of Purdue's compute resources.

model: Purdue University offers a variety of computing resources designed to meet the needs of students, faculty, and researchers. Here’s an overview of the major compute resources:

1. **Supercomputers**:
   - **Conte**: Renowned as the fastest university-owned supercomputer in the world, Conte supports high-performance computing for faculty research with capabilities of over 943 teraflops.

2. **Rosen Center for Advanced Computing (RCAC)**:
   - **Gilbreth Community Cluster**: Tailored for GPU-intensive applications, this cluster features various Nvidia Tesla GPUs (V100, A100, A10, A30) and interconnects via a 100 Gbps Infiniband network.
   - **Hammer**: Optimized for high-throughput computing, Hammer consists of multiple nodes providing extensive computational resources.
   - **Scholar Cluster**: Designed for educational purposes, it offers resources for students, including batch job submission and various software access.

3. **Departmental Resources**:
   - **Department of Statistics**: Maintains Linux servers and workstations for research and administrative tasks, featuring high RAM configurations and large storage capacities.
   - **Department of Mathematics**: Provides access to various departmental computers for research and teaching, along with Linux machines for student use.

4. **Purdue IT Facilities**:
   - **Data Depot**: A dedicated storage solution for data management.
   - **Fortress Archive**: Another storage option for long-term archiving.

5. **High-Performance Computing Services**:
   - Includes additional resources like Anvil and various community clusters tailored for specific research needs, offering access to advanced computational capabilities, data management, and job scheduling.

6. **Software Access**:
   - Users can access various applications and tools through managed resources, including scientific computing applications like MATLAB, R, and more.

For more detailed information, users can refer to Purdue's computing resources pages and user guides provided by the Research Computing services.

user: Can you tell me about bus transportation for 2025 and onwards at Purdue?

model: Purdue University's bus transportation services will undergo significant changes starting in 2025. Here are the key details:

1. **CityBus Agreement for 2024-25**:
   - For the 2024-25 academic year, Purdue University trustees approved a one-year contract with CityBus, where Purdue-affiliated riders will pay $99 per semester for off-campus services. Purdue will subsidize this cost, meaning students will only pay $25 for the semester pass. On-campus bus routes will remain free.

2. **Transition to SP Plus**:
   - Starting **July 1, 2025**, Purdue will transition to a three-year contract with SP Plus (SP+), enhancing campus transit services. This service will commence on **August 25, 2025**, aligning with the first day of fall semester classes. The SP+ service will provide free transit to students, faculty, and staff with a valid Purdue ID.

3. **Campus Transit Features**:
   - SP+ will leverage technology for improved services, which includes an enhanced mobile app, a more adaptable fleet, and expanded campus routes. Free rides will be available from **August 14, 2025**. The fleet will consist of a mixed size of low-floor buses and electric transit vans, and the service will feature fixed routes and on-demand transit options.

4. **CityBus Service After Transition**:
   - Post-July 2025, CityBus will continue to provide off-campus transit services, maintaining a relationship with Purdue to ensure students have access to affordable transportation options.

5. **Updated Shuttle Schedules**:
   - Purdue's Campus Connect commuter shuttle service offers shuttles between the West Lafayette and Indianapolis campuses, adjusting schedules as needed based on demand and operational efficiencies.

Students and staff should check Purdue’s official channels for specific schedules, routes, and any further updates as the transition progresses. If you have specific questions or need assistance, contacting Purdue's transportation services directly is recommended. 

**Warning**: This information is accurate as per the current context but is subject to change. Always refer to official university communications for the most reliable updates.

user: If I want to use city bus for off campus transit how do I set everything up?

model: To set up CityBus for off-campus transit at Purdue University, you need to follow these steps:

1. **Obtain an Off-Campus Pass**: For the fall 2025 semester, Purdue is subsidizing the cost of off-campus bus passes. The original price is $99, but with the subsidy, you will only pay $25 for the semester. You can find details about purchasing the pass on the CityBus website.

2. **Use a Valid Purdue ID**: Ensure you have a valid Purdue ID to access discounts for both on-campus and off-campus CityBus services.

3. **Plan Your Routes**: Visit the CityBus website to review the routes that connect to your desired destinations. Utilize the trip planner tools available on the site to find the best routes based on your starting point and destination.

4. **Download the CityBus App**: This app will help you track buses in real-time, check schedules, and receive alerts about service changes.

5. **Familiarize Yourself with Bus Stops**: Identify the nearest bus stop to your location and study the schedule for your specific route.

6. **Stay Updated**: Follow any updates from the CityBus regarding route changes or detours. This information is usually available through their app or website.

For further assistance, you can contact CityBus or Purdue's transportation services if you have specific questions regarding the process.

user: tell me more about the topics taught in this class

==============================
current question: tell me more about the topics taught in this class

model: The course "CS440: Large-scale Data Analytics" covers a range of topics designed to equip students with the skills necessary to analyze and derive meaningful insights from large-scale data. Here's a more detailed breakdown of the tentative topics included in the course:

1. **Database Fundamentals:** Understand the core concepts of databases, including structures, types, and functions.

2. **Big Data Storage:** Explore storage solutions for big data, particularly focusing on technologies like HDFS (Hadoop Distributed File System).

3. **Big Data Computing Frameworks:** Gain hands-on experience with frameworks such as Hadoop and Spark for processing large data sets efficiently.

4. **Data Warehouses and Data Lakes:** Learn about the architecture and use cases for storing and retrieving data in warehouses and lakes.

5. **Graph Analytics:** Study the analysis of graph structures using tools like Spark Graph, enabling insights from interconnected data.

6. **Data Streaming:** Understand the principles of real-time data processing with systems like Spark Streaming.

7. **Large-scale Machine Learning:** Delve into machine learning techniques that scale effectively to handle large datasets, particularly using Spark MLlib.

8. **Vector Databases:** Learn about advanced storage mechanisms designed for high-dimensional data.

9. **Cloud-native Data Analytics:** Explore analytics solutions built for cloud environments, integrating flexibility and scalability in data processing.

These topics are designed to provide both theoretical knowledge and practical skills crucial for working with large-scale data. As this is a tentative list, details may be subject to adjustment throughout the semester. Always refer to class communications for the most accurate and updated information.

==============================
current question: Tell me about CS44100

model: CS 44100, titled "Data Science Capstone," is a course offered by Purdue University's Department of Computer Science during the Spring 2026 semester. Here are the key details:

- **CRN:** 20433
- **Prerequisites:** CS 373
- **Instructor:** Professor Mary Anne Smart (Email: [masmart@purdue.edu](mailto:masmart@purdue.edu))
- **Office Hours:** Tuesday, 2:30 PM – 3:30 PM in DSAI 1119D or by appointment.
- **Lectures:** Mondays and Wednesdays from 4:30 PM to 5:45 PM in Hicks Undergraduate Library G980D. Class sessions will include project meetings and collaboration opportunities.
- **Course Description:** This capstone course allows students to integrate their knowledge to identify and solve real-world data science problems, using the CRISP-DM methodology for team projects.

### Learning Objectives
Students will learn to:
- Identify business objectives in complex problems.
- Develop data science goals and project plans.
- Implement data pre-processing and analysis models.
- Evaluate models and interpret results concerning business objectives.
- Present project outcomes effectively.

### Grading
- **Team Participation:** 80% (includes various reports).
- **Individual Participation:** 20% (includes presentations and class participation).

### Policies
- **Late Submission:** 5% penalty per 24-hour period, with a maximum late submission window of three days.
- **Regrade Requests:** Must be made within seven days of receiving a grade.

### Additional Resources
- The course does not have an official textbook, but several recommended readings are listed.
- Students are encouraged to utilize campus resources for mental health, disability services, and basic needs security.

For more detailed information, including specific schedules and communication channels, please refer to the course materials available on Purdue Brightspace.

However, they are allowed for the individual application project associated with the course. Any changes to this policy will be communicated to the students.

user: tell me about cs34800

==============================
current question: tell me about cs34800

model: **CS 34800: Information Systems** is a course offered in the Spring 2026 semester at Purdue University. Here’s an overview:

### Course Information
- **Course Title:** CS 34800: Information Systems
- **CRNs:** 13399 and 35118
- **Instructional Modality:** Face-to-Face
- **Course Credit Hours:** 3
- **Prerequisites:** Students should have completed Data Structures, Discrete Structures, and possess programming experience.

### Course Description
This course introduces fundamental concepts of database management, focusing primarily on the relational model. Key topics include:
- Schema design and refinement
- Query languages
- Database application development
- Transactions and concurrency control
- Overview of NoSQL databases (including graph and document stores)
- Overview of OLAP and data warehousing

### Learning Outcomes
By the end of the course, students will:
- Design effective database schemas.
- Understand various data models.
- Pose complex database queries using SQL and NoSQL languages.
- Gain foundational knowledge in indexing.
- Understand transactions and concurrency control.
- Differentiate between OLTP and OLAP databases.

### Important Dates and Schedule
- **Midterm Exam 1:** March 10, 2026, from 8:00 PM to 9:30 PM
- **Midterm Exam 2:** April 27, 2026, from 8:00 PM to 9:30 PM
- **Project Deadlines:** Project stages have specific due dates, with variations based on assignments throughout the semester.

### Homework and Assessment
- Students are required to complete six graded homework assignments, participate in quizzes, and engage in projects that account for a portion of their final grade.
- The grading scale follows standard practices (A, B, C, etc.).

### Additional Information
- **Textbook:** Recommended: *Database System Concepts, 7th Edition* by Silberschatz, Korth, and Sudarshan.
- Communication regarding course-related issues is primarily conducted via Ed, and students are encouraged to use the designated emails for TAs and instructors for queries.

This overview provides core details about CS 34800. If you have specific questions or need more information on certain aspects, feel free to ask!

user: Tell me how to study for CS348. Give some suggestions based on the content in the class.

==============================
current question: Tell me how to study for CS348. Give some suggestions based on the content in the class.

model: To effectively study for CS 34800: Information Systems, consider the following suggestions based on the course content:

### 1. **Understand Core Concepts**
   - **Database Fundamentals:** Start by grasping the basics of the relational model, schema design, and different data models.
   - **NoSQL Systems:** Familiarize yourself with NoSQL databases, focusing on the differences between document and graph databases.

### 2. **Practice SQL and NoSQL Queries**
   - **Hands-On Practice:** Use platforms like SQL Fiddle or your local database system to practice writing complex SQL queries. Try different queries to explore data extraction.
   - **NoSQL Exercises:** Explore online resources that offer NoSQL query languages, such as MongoDB or Neo4j.

### 3. **Study Schema Design**
   - **Design Exercises:** Work on exercises that involve creating and refining database schemas. This helps in understanding the structure and relationships between data.

### 4. **Engage with Course Materials**
   - **Textbook Reviews:** Regularly read and review chapters from *Database System Concepts, 7th Edition*. Focus on the sections that align with the course topics.
   - **Class Notes:** Review notes from lectures and highlight key areas discussed.

### 5. **Prepare for Exams**
   - **Midterms Focus:** For upcoming midterms, create a study guide summarizing key concepts, definitions, and examples. Pay special attention to topics noted in class.
   - **Past Exams/Quizzes:** If available, practice with past exam questions or quiz materials.

### 6. **Collaborative Learning**
   - **Study Groups:** Form a study group with classmates to discuss challenging topics and quiz each other on key concepts.
   - **Ed Discussion Board:** Use the Ed platform to ask questions and clarify doubts with peers and TAs.

### 7. **Project Development Focus**
   - **Project Planning:** Break down project requirements into manageable tasks and set deadlines for each stage. This will help you stay organized and reduce last-minute stress.
   - **Seek Feedback:** Regularly get feedback on your project work from peers or TAs to ensure you're on the right track.

### 8. **Time Management**
   - **Regular Study Schedule:** Create a weekly study plan that allocates time for reviewing lecture materials, practicing problems, and working on assignments.

By utilizing these strategies, you'll be well-prepared for CS 34800 and able to engage deeply with the course content. Good luck with your studies!

### Course Focus
- The course emphasizes the fundamental concepts of database management using the relational model. This includes practical applications like designing and using databases for web or mobile applications.

- One significant aspect of the course is the individual application project, where students develop a small database-backed website. This project allows for hands-on experience in applying concepts learned in class.

### Weekly Quizzes
- The course includes weekly quizzes (typically on Sundays) that contribute to the final grade. Students can take each quiz up to three times, with the highest score counted. This format encourages mastery of the material.

### Grading and Participation
- Participation through IClicker Cloud contributes to 3% of the final grade. This incentivizes students to engage during lectures actively.

### Resource Availability
- Lecture slides and recordings are available on Brightspace, providing an excellent resource for review. There is also a recommended textbook that offers additional materials.

### Strict Academic Integrity Policies
- There are strict policies regarding academic integrity, including checks for plagiarism in assignments. This emphasizes the importance of original work and ethical conduct.

### Interaction and Support
- Students are encouraged to ask questions on Ed, fostering a collaborative learning environment. TAs and the instructor are available for additional support during office hours.

current question: how about 180?

model: ### CS 18000 - Problem Solving and Object-Oriented Programming

**Course Overview:**
- **Credit Hours:** 4.00
- This course focuses on problem-solving and algorithms, with a strong emphasis on implementing algorithms in a high-level programming language (Java). 

**Key Topics Covered:**
- Conditionals and iterative approaches.
- Debugging and data collections.
- Searching and sorting algorithms.
- Problem decomposition.
- Object-oriented programming fundamentals including subclassing and exception handling.
- Graphical User Interfaces (GUIs) and file data storage.
- Overview of advanced topics from other computer science courses.

**Restrictions:**
- Students cannot receive credit for both CS 18000 and CS 15600, CS 15800, or CS 15900.
- The course is not open to students with credit in CS 24000.

**Course Format:**
- Typically offered in Fall, Spring, and Summer semesters.
- Includes practical components and assessments like quizzes, projects, and exams to solidify the understanding of programming principles.

**Proficiency Exam:**
- There is a proficiency exam available for students wishing to test out of this course, but it has a low passing rate (about 10%).

- **Credit Hours:** 3.00

user: what courses should I take as a CS Student second year?

==============================
current question: what courses should I take as a CS Student second year?

model: As a second-year Computer Science student at Purdue University, you should focus on core courses and electives that build on your foundational knowledge from your first year. Here’s a recommended course sequence:

### Core Courses
1. **CS 24000** - Programming in C (3 credits)
2. **CS 25100** - Data Structures and Algorithms (3 credits)
3. **CS 25000** - Computer Architecture (4 credits)
4. **CS 25200** - Systems Programming (4 credits)

### Mathematics Requirements
- **MA 26100** - Multivariate Calculus (3 credits)
- **STAT 35500** - Statistics for Data Science (3 credits) or another approved statistics course

### General Education Requirements
- A few introductory general education courses based on your interests (totaling about 6-9 credits).

### Electives
- Choose electives that align with your interests within Computer Science or related fields, such as:
  - **CS 30700** - Software Engineering I
  - **CS 34800** - Information Systems
  - **CS 37300** - Data Mining and Machine Learning
  - **CS 38100** - Introduction to the Analysis of Algorithms

### Additional Recommendations
- Participate in seminars or workshops offered by the department.
- Engage in group projects or coding competitions to enhance practical experience.

### Important Note
Make sure to meet with your academic advisor to tailor your course choices to your specific interests, track requirements, and career goals. If you need more personalized advice, don't hesitate to ask!

4. **CS 25200** - Systems Programming (4 credits)

### Mathematics Requirements
- **MA 26100** - Multivariate Calculus (3 credits)
- **STAT 35500** - Statistics for Data Science (3 credits) or another approved statistics course

### General Education Requirements
- A few introductory general education courses based on your interests (totaling about 6-9 credits).

### Electives
- Choose electives that align with your interests within Computer Science or related fields, such as:
  - **CS 30700** - Software Engineering I
  - **CS 34800** - Information Systems
  - **CS 37300** - Data Mining and Machine Learning
  - **CS 38100** - Introduction to the Analysis of Algorithms

### Additional Recommendations
- Participate in seminars or workshops offered by the department.
- Engage in group projects or coding competitions to enhance practical experience.

### Important Note
Make sure to meet with your academic advisor to tailor your course choices to your specific interests, track requirements, and career goals. If you need more personalized advice, don't hesitate to ask!

current question: Tell me about CS440. Everything you have.

model: Certainly! Here's a comprehensive overview of CS440: Large-scale Data Analytics offered at Purdue University for Spring 2026.

### Course Description
CS440 focuses on the concepts, design principles, and systems involved in analyzing large-scale data to extract transformative insights. The course will cover various aspects of "big data" and its associated technologies.

### Tentative Topics Include:
- Database fundamentals
- Big data storage (e.g., HDFS)
- Big data computing frameworks (e.g., Hadoop and Spark)
- Data warehouses
- Data lakes
- Graph analytics (e.g., Spark Graph)
- Data streaming (e.g., Spark Streaming)
- Large-scale machine learning (e.g., Spark MLlib)
- Vector databases
- Cloud-native data analytics

### Instructor
**Jianguo Wang**  
- **Email:** [csjgwang@purdue.edu](mailto:csjgwang@purdue.edu) (Please include "[CS440]" in the subject)

### Teaching Assistants
- Shige Liu: [liu3529@purdue.edu](mailto:liu3529@purdue.edu)
- Yunan Zhang: [zhan4404@purdue.edu](mailto:zhan4404@purdue.edu)
- Jiayi Liu: [liu4127@purdue.edu](mailto:liu4127@purdue.edu)

### Logistics
- **When:** Mondays and Wednesdays from **3:30 PM - 4:20 PM**
- **Where:** Wilmeth Active Learning Center 3087
- **Office Hours:** After class or by appointment
- **Pre-requisites:** CS242, CS251, and CS373

### Labs and PSOs
Labs and Problem-Solving Opportunities (PSOs) will commence from the **3rd week** of the semester.

**Lab Schedule:**
- L01: Tuesday 1:30 PM - 3:20 PM (LWSN B131)
- L02: Friday 9:30 AM - 11:20 AM (LWSN B131)
- L03: Wednesday 9:30 AM - 11:20 AM (LWSN B131)
- L04: Wednesday 11:30 AM - 1:20 PM (LWSN B131)

### Online Communications
- **Piazza:** Used for announcements, discussions, and Q&A.
- **Gradescope:** For submitting and grading homeworks.

### Textbooks (Optional)
- *Spark: The Definitive Guide* by Bill Chambers and Matei Zaharia
- *Database System Concepts* (7th edition) by Avi Silberschatz, Henry F. Korth, and S. Sudarshan
- *Mining of Massive Datasets* by Jure Leskovec, Anand Rajaraman, and Jeff Ullman

### Grading Breakdown
- Homeworks: 20% (2 assignments, each 10%)
- Midterm Exam: 25%
- Final Exam: 35%
- Project: 20% (2 projects, each 10%)
- Extra Credits: 5%

### Academic Integrity
Students are expected to uphold integrity and must familiarize themselves with the academic integrity policies provided by the university.

### Schedule of Lectures
- **Lec 1 (01/12):** Course Introduction
- **Lec 2 (01/14):** Relational DB
- **Lec 3 (01/19):** No class due to MLK Day
- **Lec 4 (01/21):** No class due to conference trip
- **Lec 5 (01/26):** SQL
- **Lec 6 (01/28):** SQL 2
- **Lec 7 (02/02):** Database Storage
- **Lec 8 (02/04):** Index
- **Lec 9 (02/09):** Query Processing
- **Lec 10 (02/11):** Query Processing 2
- **Lec 11 (02/16):** Transaction
- **Lec 12 (02/18):** Concurrency Control

user: Tell me about CS440. Everything you have.

model: Certainly! Here's a comprehensive overview of CS440: Large-scale Data Analytics offered at Purdue University for Spring 2026.

### Course Description
CS440 focuses on the concepts, design principles, and systems involved in analyzing large-scale data to extract transformative insights. The course will cover various aspects of "big data" and its associated technologies.

### Tentative Topics Include:
- Database fundamentals
- Big data storage (e.g., HDFS)
- Big data computing frameworks (e.g., Hadoop and Spark)
- Data warehouses
- Data lakes
- Graph analytics (e.g., Spark Graph)
- Data streaming (e.g., Spark Streaming)
- Large-scale machine learning (e.g., Spark MLlib)
- Vector databases
- Cloud-native data analytics

### Instructor
**Jianguo Wang**  
- **Email:** [csjgwang@purdue.edu](mailto:csjgwang@purdue.edu) (Please include "[CS440]" in the subject)

==============================
current question: Tell me about cs180

model: ### CS 18000 - Problem Solving And Object-Oriented Programming

**Credit Hours:** 4.00

**Course Description:**  
CS 18000 focuses on problem-solving and algorithms, implementing these algorithms in a high-level programming language, primarily Java. The course covers various topics, including:
- Conditionals
- Iterative approaches
- Debugging
- Data collections
- Searching and sorting algorithms
- Problem decomposition
- Object-oriented programming (OOP)
- Subclassing and exception handling
- Graphical user interfaces (GUIs)
- File data storage
- Abstract data types

This course is primarily designed for students majoring in computer sciences.

**Restrictions:**
- Credit cannot be obtained for both CS 18000 and CS 15600, CS 15800, or CS 15900.
- Not open to students with credit in CS 24000.

**Availability:**  
CS 18000 is typically offered in the Fall, Spring, and Summer semesters.

### Proficiency Exam
For students wishing to test out of CS 18000, a Proficiency Exam is available, typically scheduled for the week before or the first week of classes each semester. Success on this exam is crucial, as passing is often required for those who opt not to take the course.

**Preparing for the Exam:**
Students are encouraged to review class materials and recommended texts to prepare effectively.

### Contact Information
- **Phone:** (765) 494-4600
- **Email for Accessibility Help:** registrar@purdue.edu

If you need more specific details or have questions about prerequisites or course materials, feel free to ask!

user: How do I access Purdue's HPC resources?

==============================
current question: How do I access Purdue's HPC resources?

model: To access Purdue's High-Performance Computing (HPC) resources, follow these steps:

1. **Submit a Request**: Use the "Purdue IT Request" button available on the portal to submit your service requests related to HPC.

2. **Account Creation**: If you are a new user, request an account by specifying your research group (e.g., "Mathematics Department") to gain access to Purdue's clusters, including GPU and CPU resources.

3. **Accessing Clusters**: After your account is approved, you can access the HPC clusters, such as Gilbreth and Negishi, through designated URLs. You'll need to utilize SSH commands for remote login and may require VPN access for off-campus connections.

4. **Utilize Software**: Once you have access, you can use various applications including MATLAB and Jupyter Notebook available on the clusters.

Make sure to consult the specific user guides for additional details on software usage and job running procedures once you have access.

Please note that if you have further questions, you can directly report issues or seek assistance through the RCAC Service Request form provided on the portal. 

If you have any specific needs or encounter difficulties, it's best to reach out to the support staff directly for guidance.

2026-01-14 05:38:33
user: How do I access Purdue's HPC resources?

==============================
current question: How do I access Purdue's HPC resources?

model: To access Purdue's High-Performance Computing (HPC) resources, follow these steps:

1. **Log In to the Cluster**:
   Use the following command to log in:
   ```
   <username>@gilbreth.rcac.purdue.edu
   ```
   You will need your career account password and Duo's 2FA for authentication.

2. **Create an SSH Key (Optional)**:
   If you want to avoid using 2FA every time, create an SSH key for secure authentication. After creating the key pair, append your public key to `~/.ssh/authorized_keys`. You can use the following command:
   ```
   curl https://github.com/<username>.keys >> ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
   ```
   Alternatively, run this from your local session:
   ```
   ssh-copy-id <username>@<cluster-address>
   ```

3. **Configure Storage**:
   Be aware that your home directory on the cluster is limited to 25GB. You can use `/scratch/gilbreth/<username>` for larger storage, which should be configured for items like cache and datasets.

4. **Run Commands**:
   Once logged in, you'll have access to a shell to execute commands and submit jobs using SLURM.

5. **Utilize SLURM**:
   Submit or monitor jobs using SLURM commands, such as `sbatch` for submitting scripts, `squeue` to check job status, and `scancel` to cancel jobs.

Ensure you refer to the specific guidelines and commands for your workload as detailed in the HPC Workshop documentation.

2026-01-14 06:17:00
user: How do I access Purdue's HPC resources?

==============================
current question: How do I access Purdue's HPC resources?

model: To access Purdue's HPC resources, you need to follow these steps:

1. **Log In to the Cluster**: Use the command:
   ```
   <username>@gilbreth.rcac.purdue.edu
   ```
   Enter your career account password and use Duo's 2FA for authentication.

2. **SSH Key Setup** (Optional but recommended): Create an SSH key for easier access without needing 2FA each time. After generating the key pair, add your public key to `~/.ssh/authorized_keys` on the cluster.

3. **Configuration for Cache**: Since your home directory has a 25GB limit, it's advisable to store large items in scratch space. You can redirect your cache to `/scratch/gilbreth/<username>/` by updating your `~/.bashrc` with:
   ```
   export XDG_CACHE_HOME=/scratch/gilbreth/<username>/
   ```
   Then, run `source ~/.bashrc` to apply the changes.

4. **Using SLURM for Job Management**: After logging in, you can use SLURM to submit jobs. You can configure your jobs with various constraints (like walltime, resource types) using the `sbatch` command along with a bash script that sets these parameters.

5. **Interactive Jobs**: For testing configurations, you can run interactive jobs with:
   ```
   sinteractive -A <account> -N <number of nodes> -n <number of tasks> -c <cpus/task> -G <number of GPUs> -t <job walltime>
   ```

6. **Monitoring Your Job**: Check the job status using:
   ```
   squeue -u <username>
   ```

For any detailed specifics, refer to the manual provided for SLURM or the available resources.

2026-01-14 06:25:34
user: How do I access Purdue's HPC resources?

==============================
current question: How do I access Purdue's HPC resources?

model: To access Purdue's HPC resources, follow these steps:

1. **Log in to the Cluster**:
   Use the following command to log into the cluster:
   ```
   <username>@gilbreth.rcac.purdue.edu
   ```
   You'll need your career account password and Duo's 2FA for authentication.

2. **Configure SSH Key (Optional)**:
   To avoid using 2FA each time, you can create and add an SSH Key for secure authentication. After creating a key pair, append the public key to `~/.ssh/authorized_keys`. You can do this by running:
   ```
   curl https://github.com/<username>.keys >> ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
   ```
   Alternatively, use:
   ```
   ssh-copy-id <username>@<cluster-address>
   ```

3. **Use the SLURM Utility**:
   SLURM is the cluster management utility for submitting jobs. Familiarize yourself with its commands for job submission and monitoring.

4. **Configure Storage**:
   Due to the 25GB limit on your home directory, consider using the scratch space at `/scratch/gilbreth/<username>` for larger datasets or cache.

5. **Prepare Runtime Environment**:
   Load necessary modules and set up Python dependencies to ensure your environment is configured correctly.

Refer to the detailed steps above to get everything set up effectively.

user: can you explain stochastic calculus to me?

==============================
current question: can you explain stochastic calculus to me?

model: Stochastic calculus is a branch of mathematics that deals with processes involving random variables and is often used in fields such as finance, physics, and engineering.

At its core, stochastic calculus extends traditional calculus to include randomness, facilitating the analysis of systems that evolve over time with inherent uncertainty. One of the most important concepts is the Itô integral, which allows for integration with respect to stochastic processes like Brownian motion.

Key components of stochastic calculus include:

1. **Stochastic Processes**: Functions that describe a system whose state changes probabilistically over time, such as stock prices.
  
2. **Itô's Lemma**: This is often considered the fundamental theorem of stochastic calculus. It provides a way to differentiate and integrate functions of stochastic processes.

3. **Brownian Motion**: A continuous-time stochastic process that models random motion, crucial in defining various stochastic calculus concepts.

4. **Applications**: Stochastic calculus is particularly important in financial mathematics, for pricing options and managing risks.

If you need further clarification or more specific details, feel free to ask!

user: Tell me all you know about cs34800

==============================
current question: Tell me all you know about cs34800

model: ### CS 34800: Information Systems - Spring 2026 Overview

#### Course Information
- **Course Title:** CS 34800: Information Systems
- **CRNs:** 13399 and 35118
- **Instructional Modality:** Face-to-Face
- **Course Credit Hours:** 3
- **Prerequisites:** Data Structures, Discrete Structures, and programming experience.

#### Course Description
This course introduces the fundamental concepts of database management, primarily through the relational model, covering topics such as:
- Schema design and refinement
- Query languages (SQL and NoSQL)
- Database application development
- Transactions and concurrency control
- Overview of NoSQL databases and OLAP

#### Homework and Exam Policies
- **General:** 
  - Six graded homework assignments.
  - Use LaTeX, MS Word, or comparable editors for submissions, only in PDF format on Gradescope.
  - Late homework accepted within 72 hours of the due date with penalties.

- **Regrade Requests:** 
  - Submit within three days with a clear explanation.

- **Exams:** 
  - Two midterm exams, open-book and open-note, with no electronic devices allowed.
  
#### Quizzes
- Weekly quizzes are auto-graded; the highest score of up to three attempts counts.

#### Participation and Attendance
- Class attendance is not recorded, but in-class participation through IClicker contributes to the course grade.

#### Technical Issues
- Report IClicker issues directly to the instructor via email with relevant details.

#### Course Schedule Highlights
- **Week 1:**
  - Jan 13 (West Lafayette), Jan 12 (Indianapolis) - Introduction to relational databases and SQL.
  
- **Midterms:**
  - Midterm Exam 1: **March 10, 2026, at 8:00 PM**
  - Midterm Exam 2: **April 27, 2026, at 8:00 PM**

#### Final Assessments
- No final exam; project stages due during the final week.

#### Instructor and TAs
- **Instructor:** Hisham Benotman
- **Lead TA:** Xi Pang
  
#### Communication
- Preferred communication via Ed for questions and announcements.

#### Important Notes
- Purdue University emphasizes academic integrity, and violations can lead to severe consequences.
- AI and LLM tools are prohibited in assignments but allowed for the application project.

For more specifics, refer to the detailed syllabus and the course schedule link provided at the beginning of the semester.

---

If you have specific queries regarding assignments, I recommend checking the syllabus or the Ed platform for the most up-to-date information.

- Overview of NoSQL databases and OLAP

#### Homework and Exam Policies
- **General:** 
  - Six graded homework assignments.
  - Use LaTeX, MS Word, or comparable editors for submissions, only in PDF format on Gradescope.
  - Late homework accepted within 72 hours of the due date with penalties.

- **Regrade Requests:** 
  - Submit within three days with a clear explanation.

- **Exams:** 
  - Two midterm exams, open-book and open-note, with no electronic devices allowed.
  
#### Quizzes
- Weekly quizzes are auto-graded; the highest score of up to three attempts counts.

#### Participation and Attendance
- Class attendance is not recorded, but in-class participation through IClicker contributes to the course grade.

#### Technical Issues
- Report IClicker issues directly to the instructor via email with relevant details.

#### Course Schedule Highlights
- **Week 1:**
  - Jan 13 (West Lafayette), Jan 12 (Indianapolis) - Introduction to relational databases and SQL.
  
- **Midterms:**
  - Midterm Exam 1: **March 10, 2026, at 8:00 PM**
  - Midterm Exam 2: **April 27, 2026, at 8:00 PM**

#### Final Assessments
- No final exam; project stages due during the final week.

#### Instructor and TAs
- **Instructor:** Hisham Benotman
- **Lead TA:** Xi Pang
  
#### Communication
- Preferred communication via Ed for questions and announcements.

#### Important Notes
- Purdue University emphasizes academic integrity, and violations can lead to severe consequences.
- AI and LLM tools are prohibited in assignments but allowed for the application project.

For more specifics, refer to the detailed syllabus and the course schedule link provided at the beginning of the semester.

If you have specific queries regarding assignments, I recommend checking the syllabus or the Ed platform for the most up-to-date information.

current question: Give me information about CS441

model: The course you're inquiring about is **CS 44100: Data Science Capstone** for the Spring 2026 semester at Purdue University. 

### Key Details:
- **Instructor**: Professor Mary Anne Smart  
- **Email**: [masmart@purdue.edu](mailto:masmart@purdue.edu)  
- **Office Hours**: Tuesdays, 2:30 PM – 3:30 PM in DSAI 1119D or by appointment  
- **Lecture Schedule**: Mondays and Wednesdays from 4:30 PM to 5:45 PM in Hicks Undergraduate Library G980D  

### Course Description:
This capstone course integrates your accumulated knowledge and skills to identify and solve realistic data science problems, focusing on practical applications. Projects may be sponsored by corporate partners or academic/non-academic research groups, utilizing the CRISP-DM methodology. 

### Course Structure:
1. **Introduction (2 weeks)**: Discuss CRISP-DM and project selection.
2. **Business and Data Understanding (4 weeks)**: Explore projects and plan necessary information.
3. **Data Preparation (3 weeks)**: Format data for analysis.
4. **Modeling (4 weeks)**: Experiment with modeling techniques.
5. **Evaluation (Remainder of Semester)**: Assess model effectiveness and outcomes.

### Assessments:
- **Team Participation (80%)**
  - Various reports including Business Understanding, Data Understanding, Data Preparation, Modeling, and Evaluation.
- **Individual Participation (20%)**
  - Team project presentations and attendance.

### Important Dates:
- **Week 1**: Course introduction on January 12 and 14
- **MLK Day**: No class on January 19
- **Spring Break**: March 16 - March 18
- **Final Presentations**: Scheduled end of April

### Contact Information for Assistance:
- **Graduate Teaching Assistants**:
  - Sai Coumar: [scoumar@purdue.edu](mailto:scoumar@purdue.edu)  
  - Shrutheesh Iyer: [iyer270@purdue.edu](mailto:iyer270@purdue.edu)  
  - Pinaki Mohanty: [pmohanty@purdue.edu](mailto:pmohanty@purdue.edu)  

### Additional Resources:
- Brightspace course platform for materials and updates.

Feel free to ask if you need more specific information or details!

user: tell me how to access HPC

==============================
current question: tell me how to access HPC

model: To access Purdue's High-Performance Computing (HPC) resources, follow these steps:

1. **Log in to the Cluster**: Use the following command in your terminal:
   ```
   <username>@gilbreth.rcac.purdue.edu
   ```
   Enter your career account password and complete Duo's 2FA for authentication.

2. **Set Up SSH Key** (Optional): To avoid using 2FA for every login, create an SSH key:
   - Generate a key pair and add the public key to `~/.ssh/authorized_keys`.
   - You can add your SSH keys to GitHub with this command:
     ```
     curl https://github.com/<username>.keys >> ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
     ```
   - Alternatively, you can run:
     ```
     ssh-copy-id <username>@<cluster-address>
     ```

3. **Use SLURM for Job Management**: Once logged in, you can use SLURM commands to submit and manage jobs. Make sure to review the job specifications such as walltime, resources, and account type.

4. **Monitor Your Jobs**: Check the status of your jobs by running:
   ```
   squeue -u <username>
   ```

For additional resources or details, refer to the [RCAC SLURM Guide](#) or the [SLURM Documentation](#).

Make sure to follow all guidelines regarding resource usage and storage limits.

user: explain to me each step in detail

==============================
current question: explain to me each step in detail

model: To access Purdue's High-Performance Computing (HPC) resources, you need to follow a series of detailed steps. Below is the comprehensive explanation for each step:

### 1. Log in to the Cluster

To begin using the HPC resources, you need to log into the cluster:

- **Command**:
   ```
   <username>@gilbreth.rcac.purdue.edu
   ```
- **Explanation**:
   Replace `<username>` with your actual Purdue career account username. This command uses the SSH (Secure Shell) protocol to connect to the cluster. Once executed, you will be prompted to enter your career account password. 

- **Duo 2FA**: 
   After typing your password, you may need to complete Duo's two-factor authentication to verify your identity. Follow the on-screen instructions to authenticate your login.

### 2. Set Up SSH Key (Optional)

To simplify future logins, you can set up an SSH key. This eliminates the need to enter your Duo authentication every time you access the cluster.

- **Generate SSH Key**:
   - Use the following command to generate an SSH key pair (if you haven't already):
     ```
     ssh-keygen -t rsa -b 4096 -C "your_email@example.com"
     ```
   - Follow the prompts to save the key (default is usually acceptable) and set a passphrase if desired.

- **Add Public Key**:
   - Append your public key to the `~/.ssh/authorized_keys` file on the cluster by running:
     ```
     curl https://github.com/<username>.keys >> ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
     ```
     This command assumes you have added your SSH keys to GitHub.

   - Alternatively, you can automatically append your public SSH key using:
     ```
     ssh-copy-id <username>@gilbreth.rcac.purdue.edu
     ```

### 3. Use SLURM for Job Management

Once logged in, SLURM (Simple Linux Utility for Resource Management) is used to submit and manage jobs on the cluster.

- **Submitting a Job**:
   - You will typically create a batch script that defines parameters for your job, like:
     ```bash
     #!/bin/bash
     #SBATCH --job-name=my_job
     #SBATCH --output=output.txt
     #SBATCH --error=error.txt
     #SBATCH --time=01:00:00
     #SBATCH --ntasks=1
     #SBATCH --cpus-per-task=4
     #SBATCH --gres=gpu:1

     # Load required modules
     module load cuda anaconda

     # Run your application
     python my_script.py
     ```
   - Once your script is ready, submit it using:
     ```
     sbatch path/to/script.sh
     ```
   - **Parameters Explained**:
     - `#SBATCH --job-name`: Name of your job.
     - `#SBATCH --output`: Where to write standard output.
     - `#SBATCH --error`: Where to write error messages.
     - `#SBATCH --time`: Maximum runtime (format: HH:MM:SS).
     - `#SBATCH --ntasks`: Number of tasks to run.
     - `#SBATCH --cpus-per-task`: Number of CPUs per task.
     - `#SBATCH --gres`: General Resource specification, like GPUs.

### 4. Monitor Your Jobs

You can track the status of your submitted jobs to know if they are running, completed, or failed.

- **Check Status**:
   ```
   squeue -u <username>
   ```
- **Explanation**:
   This command will show all jobs you've submitted using SLURM, displaying details such as job status (running, pending, etc.), job ID, and other relevant information.

- **Checking Available Resources**:
   - To check the available resources in your account:
     ```
     qlist
     ```

- **Log Into Running Jobs**:
   If you need to access a job that's currently running:
   ```
   srun --jobid=<jobid> --pty /usr/bin/bash -i
   ```

- **Cancelling Jobs**:
   If you need to cancel a job:
   ```
   scancel <jobid>
   ```

### Additional Notes

- Ensure that you follow guidelines regarding resource usage and storage limits. For instance, your home directory has a limit of 25GB, and it's recommended to store large datasets in `/scratch/gilbreth/<username>` to avoid exceeding this limit.

- Reference the provided resources like the [RCAC SLURM Guide](#) or the [SLURM Documentation](#) for more detailed assistance.

By following these detailed steps, you should be able to effectively log in and utilize Purdue's HPC resources for your computational needs. If you have any further questions or need clarification on any steps, feel free to ask!

user: Give me the schedule for CS381

==============================
current question: Give me the schedule for CS381

model: Here’s the schedule for CS381: Fundamental Algorithms in Spring 2026 at Purdue University:

### Course Schedule

#### Key Dates and Topics
- **Week 1:**  
  - January 13: Searching and sorting, including binary search, merge sort, and lower bounds for sorting (Chapter 1).  
  - January 15: Induction and recursion (Chapter 2).  
  - January 20: Selection and closest pair in R² (Chapter 3).  
  - **Homework 0 due**: January 22.

- **Week 2:**  
  - January 22: Fast integer multiplication and Fourier transform (Chapter 4).  
  - January 27: Edit distance and matrix chain multiplication (Chapter 5).  
  - January 29: Depth-first search and strongly connected components (Chapter 6).  
  - **Homework 1 due**: January 29.

- **Week 3:**  
  - February 3: Breadth-first search and single-source shortest paths (Chapter 7).  
  - February 5: Negative edge weights and all-pairs shortest paths (Chapter 8).  
  - **Homework 2 due**: February 5.  
  - February 10: Boolean satisfiability, CNF satisfiability, and reduction from Boolean to CNF SAT (Chapter 9).  
  - February 12: 2-SAT and Max 2-SAT (Chapter 10).  
  - **Midterm Exam 1**: February 18, 8–10 PM.

- **Week 4:**  
  - February 24: Subset sum (Chapter 11).  
  - February 26: Hamiltonian path on graphs and on DAGs (Chapter 12).  
  - **Homework 3 due**: February 12.  
  - **Homework 4 due**: February 26.

- **Week 5:**  
  - March 3: Independent set, coloring, and optimization problems on graphs (Chapter 13).  
  - March 5: NP-Completeness and big-picture summary (Chapter 14).  
  - **Homework 5 due**: March 5.

- **Week 6:**  
  - March 10: Spanning trees (Chapter 16).  
  - March 12: Disjoint paths and min-cut.  
  - **Spring Break**: March 17–19 - No classes.

- **Week 7:**  
  - March 24: Network flow (Chapter 18).  
  - March 26: Applications of disjoint paths and network flow (Chapter 19).  
  - **Homework 6 due**: March 12.  
  - **Homework 7 due**: March 26.

- **Week 8:**  
  - April 1: Midterm Exam 2, 8–10 PM.  
  - April 7: Lazy data structures and amortized analysis.

current question: Do I need to learn latex to do homework?

model: Yes, you are expected to typeset your homework submissions in LaTeX or an equivalent format. While it may not be strictly mandatory at the outset, learning LaTeX is highly beneficial, especially if you plan to continue in academia or pursue graduate studies. The course materials specify the need for mathematical and scientific formatting standards, which LaTeX is well-suited for. If you're unfamiliar with it, consider using resources like Overleaf or Marktext to get started.

  - January 29: Depth-first search and strongly connected components (Chapter 6).  
  - **Homework 1 due**: January 29.

- **Week 3:**  
  - February 3: Breadth-first search and single-source shortest paths (Chapter 7).  
  - February 5: Negative edge weights and all-pairs shortest paths (Chapter 8).  
  - **Homework 2 due**: February 5.  
  - February 10: Boolean satisfiability, CNF satisfiability, and reduction from Boolean to CNF SAT (Chapter 9).  
  - February 12: 2-SAT and Max 2-SAT (Chapter 10).  
  - **Midterm Exam 1**: February 18, 8–10 PM.

- **Week 4:**  
  - February 24: Subset sum (Chapter 11).  
  - February 26: Hamiltonian path on graphs and on DAGs (Chapter 12).  
  - **Homework 3 due**: February 12.  
  - **Homework 4 due**: February 26.

- **Week 5:**  
  - March 3: Independent set, coloring, and optimization problems on graphs (Chapter 13).  
  - March 5: NP-Completeness and big-picture summary (Chapter 14).  
  - **Homework 5 due**: March 5.

- **Week 6:**  
  - March 10: Spanning trees (Chapter 16).  
  - March 12: Disjoint paths and min-cut.  
  - **Spring Break**: March 17–19 - No classes.

- **Week 7:**  
  - March 24: Network flow (Chapter 18).  
  - March 26: Applications of disjoint paths and network flow (Chapter 19).  
  - **Homework 6 due**: March 12.  
  - **Homework 7 due**: March 26.

- **Week 8:**  
  - April 1: Midterm Exam 2, 8–10 PM.  
  - April 7: Lazy data structures and amortized analysis.  
  - April 9: Randomized searching and sorting (Chapter 24).  
  - **Homework 8 due**: April 9.

- **Week 9:**  
  - April 14: The push-relabel algorithm for flow (Chapter 23).  
  - April 16: Randomized rounding (Chapter 25).  
  - **Homework 9 due**: April 16.  
  - **Homework 10 due**: April 23.

- **Week 10:**  
  - April 21: Hashing and heavy hitters (Chapter 26).  
  - April 23: Matchings (Chapter 21).  
  - April 28: TBD.  
  - April 30: TBD.

- **Final Exam**: TBD (There is no final exam for this course).

user: Give me the schedule for CS381

model: Here’s the schedule for CS381: Fundamental Algorithms in Spring 2026 at Purdue University:

### Course Schedule

#### Key Dates and Topics
- **Week 1:**  
  - January 13: Searching and sorting, including binary search, merge sort, and lower bounds for sorting (Chapter 1).  
  - January 15: Induction and recursion (Chapter 2).  
  - January 20: Selection and closest pair in R² (Chapter 3).  
  - **Homework 0 due**: January 22.

- **Week 2:**  
  - January 22: Fast integer multiplication and Fourier transform (Chapter 4).  
  - January 27: Edit distance and matrix chain multiplication (Chapter 5).  
  - January 29: Depth-first search and strongly connected components (Chapter 6).  
  - **Homework 1 due**: January 29.

- **Week 3:**  
  - February 3: Breadth-first search and single-source shortest paths (Chapter 7).  
  - February 5: Negative edge weights and all-pairs shortest paths (Chapter 8).  
  - **Homework 2 due**: February 5.  
  - February 10: Boolean satisfiability, CNF satisfiability, and reduction from Boolean to CNF SAT (Chapter 9).  
  - February 12: 2-SAT and Max 2-SAT (Chapter 10).  
  - **Midterm Exam 1**: February 18, 8–10 PM.

- **Week 4:**  
  - February 24: Subset sum (Chapter 11).  
  - February 26: Hamiltonian path on graphs and on DAGs (Chapter 12).  
  - **Homework 3 due**: February 12.  
  - **Homework 4 due**: February 26.

- **Week 5:**  
  - March 3: Independent set, coloring, and optimization problems on graphs (Chapter 13).  
  - March 5: NP-Completeness and big-picture summary (Chapter 14).  
  - **Homework 5 due**: March 5.

- **Week 6:**  
  - March 10: Spanning trees (Chapter 16).  
  - March 12: Disjoint paths and min-cut.  
  - **Spring Break**: March 17–19 - No classes.

- **Week 7:**  
  - March 24: Network flow (Chapter 18).  
  - March 26: Applications of disjoint paths and network flow (Chapter 19).  
  - **Homework 6 due**: March 12.  
  - **Homework 7 due**: March 26.

- **Week 8:**  
  - April 1: Midterm Exam 2, 8–10 PM.  
  - April 7: Lazy data structures and amortized analysis.  
  - April 9: Randomized searching and sorting (Chapter 24).  
  - **Homework 8 due**: April 9.

- **Week 9:**  
  - April 14: The push-relabel algorithm for flow (Chapter 23).  
  - April 16: Randomized rounding (Chapter 25).  
  - **Homework 9 due**: April 16.  
  - **Homework 10 due**: April 23.

- **Week 10:**  
  - April 21: Hashing and heavy hitters (Chapter 26).  
  - April 23: Matchings (Chapter 21).  
  - April 28: TBD.  
  - April 30: TBD.

- **Final Exam**: TBD (There is no final exam for this course).

This schedule is subject to change, so always refer to the course syllabus for the most current information.

-------------------------------------------- # Client pool (round-robin) # -------------------------------------------- clients = [Cerebras(api_key=k) for k in API_KEYS] client_cycle = itertools.cycle(clients)  # -------------------------------------------- # Chat loop # -------------------------------------------- def chat_loop():     print("🔵 Cerebras Chat (type 'exit' or Ctrl+C to quit)")     print("-" * 50)      chat_history = []      while True:         try:             user_input = input("\nYou: ").strip()              if user_input.lower() in {"exit", "quit"}:                 print("👋 Exiting chat.")                 break              chat_history.append({"role": "user", "content": user_input})              client = next(client_cycle)              response = client.chat.completions.create(                 model=MODEL,                 messages=chat_history,                 max_completion_tokens=MAX_TOKENS,                 temperature=TEMPERATURE,                 top_p=1,                 stream=False,             )              assistant_msg = response.choices[0].message.content.strip()             chat_history.append({"role": "assistant", "content": assistant_msg})              print("\nAssistant:")             print(assistant_msg)          except KeyboardInterrupt:             print("\n👋 Interrupted. Exiting chat.")             break          except Exception as e:             print("\n⚠️ ERROR")             print("Type:", type(e).__name__)             print("Message:", e)  # -------------------------------------------- # Entry point # -------------------------------------------- if __name__ == "__main__":     chat_loop()

-------------------------------------------- # Client pool (round-robin) # -------------------------------------------- clients = [Cerebras(api_key=k) for k in API_KEYS] client_cycle = itertools.cycle(clients)  # -------------------------------------------- # Chat loop # -------------------------------------------- def chat_loop():     print("🔵 Cerebras Chat (type 'exit' or Ctrl+C to quit)")     print("-" * 50)      chat_history = []      while True:         try:             user_input = input("\nYou: ").strip()              if user_input.lower() in {"exit", "quit"}:                 print("👋 Exiting chat.")                 break              chat_history.append({"role": "user", "content": user_input})              client = next(client_cycle)              response = client.chat.completions.create(                 model=MODEL,                 messages=chat_history,                 max_completion_tokens=MAX_TOKENS,                 temperature=TEMPERATURE,                 top_p=1,                 stream=False,             )              assistant_msg = response.choices[0].message.content.strip()             chat_history.append({"role": "assistant", "content": assistant_msg})              print("\nAssistant:")             print(assistant_msg)          except KeyboardInterrupt:             print("\n👋 Interrupted. Exiting chat.")             break          except Exception as e:             print("\n⚠️ ERROR")             print("Type:", type(e).__name__)             print("Message:", e)  # -------------------------------------------- # Entry point # -------------------------------------------- if __name__ == "__main__":     chat_loop()

user: Tell me about cs348

==============================
current question: Tell me about cs348

model: CS 34800: Information Systems is a course offered in the Spring 2026 semester at Purdue University. Here are the details for this course:

### Course Information
- **Course Title:** CS 34800: Information Systems
- **CRNs:** 13399 and 35118
- **Instructional Modality:** Face-to-Face
- **Course Credit Hours:** 3
- **Prerequisites:** Data Structures, Discrete Structures, and programming experience.

### Course Description
The course introduces fundamental concepts of database management, primarily using the relational model. Key topics include:
- Schema design and refinement
- Query languages
- Database application development
- Transactions and concurrency control
- Overview of NoSQL databases (including graph and document stores)
- Overview of OLAP and data warehousing

### Assignments and Exams
- **Homework:** Six graded homework assignments will be given.
- **Quizzes:** Weekly quizzes are typically posted on Sundays.
- **Exams:** There will be two midterm evening exams, and a lab exam covering SQL content.

### Grading Breakdown
- Assignments: 10%
- Quizzes: 2%
- In-class Participation: 3%
- Lab Exam: 20%
- 1st Midterm Exam: 25%
- 2nd Midterm Exam: 30%
- Web/Mobile Application Project: 10%

### Communication and Support
- **Lead TA Contact:** Xi Pang (pang65@purdue.edu)
- Students are encouraged to use Ed for questions and to email TAs for specific issues.

If you have any specific questions or need further details about topics, assignments, or exam formats, feel free to ask!

   - For example, if you have questions regarding HW1, you can reach out to Yinqi Sun at [sun1226@purdue.edu](mailto:sun1226@purdue.edu).

3. **Instructor Office Hours**: You can also reach out to the instructor, Hisham Benotman, by scheduling an appointment via email. He is available after class for urgent questions.

4. **Course Communication Platforms**: Utilize Ed for posting questions or checking if similar inquiries have been made by classmates.

These resources will help you get the assistance you need effectively.

user: Do you have more information from the syllabus?

==============================
current question: Do you have more information from the syllabus?

model: For **CS 381: Fundamental Algorithms**, the syllabus contains several important details. Here are key points:

1. **Course Components**: 
   - The course involves lectures, homework assignments, and exams.
   - Homework assignments are due weekly.

2. **Homework Policy**:
   - Assignments are submitted online through Gradescope.
   - Late submissions are accepted up to 4 days late with a 35% penalty.
   - The bottom one-fifth of homework scores is dropped.

3. **Exams**:
   - Midterm 1: February 18, 2026, from 8 PM to 10 PM.
   - Midterm 2: April 1, 2026, from 8 PM to 10 PM.
   - Final exam date: TBD.

4. **Grading**:
   - Homework: 25%
   - Midterm 1: 20%
   - Midterm 2: 25%
   - Final: 30%

5. **Office Hours**:
   - Check the course syllabus for specific TA office hours and locations.

6. **Collaboration Policy**:
   - Students are encouraged to collaborate and discuss assignments, but all collaborators must be indicated.

7. **Academic Integrity**:
   - Strict policies against cheating and dishonesty are enforced.

For more specifics, including topics covered in lectures, it's best to refer directly to the course syllabus or the course website. If you have more specific questions in mind, feel free to ask!

user: Do you have more information from the syllabus?

model: For **CS 381: Fundamental Algorithms**, the syllabus contains several important details. Here are key points:

1. **Course Components**: 
   - The course involves lectures, homework assignments, and exams.
   - Homework assignments are due weekly.

2. **Homework Policy**:
   - Assignments are submitted online through Gradescope.
   - Late submissions are accepted up to 4 days late with a 35% penalty.
   - The bottom one-fifth of homework scores is dropped.

3. **Exams**:
   - Midterm 1: February 18, 2026, from 8 PM to 10 PM.
   - Midterm 2: April 1, 2026, from 8 PM to 10 PM.
   - Final exam date: TBD.

4. **Grading**:
   - Homework: 25%
   - Midterm 1: 20%
   - Midterm 2: 25%
   - Final: 30%

5. **Office Hours**:
   - Check the course syllabus for specific TA office hours and locations.

6. **Collaboration Policy**:
   - Students are encouraged to collaborate and discuss assignments, but all collaborators must be indicated.

7. **Academic Integrity**:
   - Strict policies against cheating and dishonesty are enforced.

For more specifics, including topics covered in lectures, it's best to refer directly to the course syllabus or the course website. If you have more specific questions in mind, feel free to ask!

### Course Information
- **Course Title:** Large-scale Data Analytics
- **Semester:** Spring 2026
- **Instructor:** Jianguo Wang  
  - **Email:** [csjgwang@purdue.edu](mailto:csjgwang@purdue.edu) (please include "[CS440]" in the subject line)
  
### Course Description
The course covers key concepts, design principles, and systems to analyze large-scale data to extract transformative insights, focusing on big data techniques.

### Tentative Topics
- Database fundamentals
- Big data storage (e.g., HDFS)
- Big data computing frameworks (e.g., Hadoop, Spark)
- Data warehouses and data lakes
- Graph analytics (e.g., Spark Graph)
- Data streaming (e.g., Spark Streaming)
- Large-scale machine learning (e.g., Spark MLlib)
- Vector databases
- Cloud-native data analytics

### Class Logistics
- **When:** Mondays and Wednesdays, 3:30 PM - 4:20 PM
- **Where:** Wilmeth Active Learning Center 3087
- **Office Hours:** After class or by appointment
- **Pre-requisites:** CS242, CS251, and CS373

user: Give me the cs348 syllabus including PSO info.

==============================
current question: Give me the cs348 syllabus including PSO info.

model: ### CS 34800: Information Systems Syllabus - Spring 2026

#### Course Information
- **Course Title:** CS 34800: Information Systems
- **CRNs:** 13399 and 35118
- **Instructional Modality:** Face-to-Face
- **Course Credit Hours:** 3
- **Prerequisites:** Data Structures, Discrete Structures, and programming experience.

#### Course Description
This course introduces fundamental concepts of database management, primarily using the relational model. Key topics include:
- Schema design and refinement
- Query languages
- Database application development
- Transactions and concurrency control
- Overview of NoSQL databases (including graph and document stores)
- Overview of OLAP and data warehousing

#### Course Comparisons
- **CS34800:** Focuses on application development scenarios.
- **CS44000:** Targets data-science and machine learning.
- **CS44800:** Concentrates on database system implementation.

#### Course Learning Outcomes
Students will learn to:
- Design effective database schemas.
- Understand different data models.
- Pose complex database queries.
- Gain foundational knowledge in indexing.
- Understand transactions and concurrency control.
- Differentiate between OLTP and OLAP databases.

#### Homework and TA Information
- **Lead TA:** Each homework will have a designated lead TA.
- **Project TA:** A project TA will be assigned for project grading.

##### Head TA Contact Information
- **Name:** Xi Pang
- **Email:** [pang65@purdue.edu](mailto:pang65@purdue.edu)

#### Instructor Information
- **Name:** Hisham Benotman
- **Email:** [hbenotma@purdue.edu](mailto:hbenotma@purdue.edu)
- **Office:** DSAI 1119G
- **Office Hours:** By appointment via email.

#### Teaching Assistants
- Xi Pang (Head TA) - [pang65@purdue.edu](mailto:pang65@purdue.edu)
- Yinqi Sun (HW1 Lead TA) - [sun1226@purdue.edu](mailto:sun1226@purdue.edu)
- Yeasir Rayhan (HW6 Lead TA) - [yrayhan@purdue.edu](mailto:yrayhan@purdue.edu)
- Additional TAs listed above.

#### TA Office Hours
Every weekday from **4:00 PM to 6:00 PM** (excluding holidays).  
Join via [Zoom link](https://purdue-edu.zoom.us/j/98460606670).  
In-person hours will be posted soon.

#### Textbook
- **Recommended:** Database System Concepts, 7th Edition by Avi Silberschatz, Henry F. Korth, and S. Sudarshan.
  - ISBN (Paperback): 978-1260084504
  - ISBN (eTextbook, hardcover, or loose-leaf): 978-0078022159

#### Lectures
- **Meeting Times:**
  - Indianapolis: **MW 5:30 PM to 6:45 PM**, ET 202.
  - West Lafayette: **TuTh 10:30 AM to 11:45 AM**, BHEE 129.

#### PSO Schedule
- PSOs will begin in the **second week of the semester**. Attendance is optional.
- **Locations:**
  - Indianapolis: SL 247
  - West Lafayette: HAAS G050
- PSO schedule will be posted at the end of week 1.

#### Important Notes
- Class announcements will be predominantly made via Ed.
- Public questions will be prioritized. For personal queries, consider rephrasing them for public discussion.

#### Additional Resources
Lecture slides and recordings will be available on Brightspace, and homework submissions via Gradescope may be required. 

For any specifics not covered in the syllabus, students can refer to the course schedule or reach out to TAs or the Head TA.

### CS38100: Introduction to the Analysis of Algorithms

#### Course Overview:
- Focus on fundamental algorithms in computer science.
- Emphasis on algorithm design and analysis.

#### Course Structure:
- **Lectures**: Scheduled twice a week.
- **Homework Assignments**: Due weekly, starting from January 22, 2026.
- **Exams**: Includes midterms and a final exam.
  
#### Grading:
- **Weight Distribution**:
  - Homework: 25%
  - Midterm 1: 20%
  - Midterm 2: 25%
  - Final Exam: 30%
  
#### Key Topics Covered:
- Searching and sorting algorithms.
- Graph algorithms and NP-Completeness.
- Dynamic programming and optimization problems.
- Randomized algorithms.

#### Policies:
- Collaboration is encouraged, but all collaborators must be indicated.
- Late submissions accepted with a penalty.
- Exemplary submissions may earn extra credit.

For the exact syllabus, including detailed topics and reading assignments, please refer to the course’s official website or contact the instructors directly. Be aware that the information is based on the provided context, and any specifics might vary. Always check with official resources to ensure accuracy.

user: Can you explain these policies in more detail?

==============================
current question: Can you explain these policies in more detail?

model: Sure! Here’s a detailed explanation of the late submission policies for CS381:

### Late Submission Policy Overview

1. **Late Days Utilization**:
   - Students can submit assignments up to **4 days late**.
   - However, submissions are subject to a **35% penalty** on the total possible points. This means whether you submit one day late or four days late, you will still incur the same penalty.

2. **Free Late Days**:
   - The course provides a certain number of **free late days**. These allow students to submit assignments after the deadline without the 35% penalty.
   - Using a free late day means the submission will receive full credit if turned in within the allowed late period.
   - Students should check the specific number of free late days provided and any conditions or application processes associated with them.

3. **Submission Format**:
   - Late submissions must adhere to the formatting requirements (e.g., LaTeX). Ensure that the quality of the submission does not suffer due to lateness.

4. **Request Process**:
   - For any clarifications or to inform TAs about using free late days, students should communicate through office hours or on platforms like EdStem, as email communication should be reserved for emergencies.

### Recommendations
- **Plan Ahead**: It’s advisable to utilize free late days judiciously and to track how many days you have remaining to avoid issues later in the semester.
- **Refer to Course Materials**: Always verify specific allowances and potential updates in policies by reviewing the course syllabus or other official communications.

If you need further clarification or have specific scenarios in mind, please feel free to ask!

user: Tell me what chapters and topics are covered in CS381 in order.

==============================
current question: Tell me what chapters and topics are covered in CS381 in order.

model: The topics and chapters covered in CS381 in order are as follows:

1. **January 13**: Searching and sorting, including binary search, merge sort, and lower bounds for sorting (Chapter 1).
2. **January 15**: Induction and recursion (Chapter 2).
3. **January 20**: Selection and closest pair in R² (Chapter 3).
4. **January 22**: Fast integer multiplication and Fourier transform (Chapter 4).
5. **January 27**: Edit distance and matrix chain multiplication (Chapter 5).
6. **January 29**: Depth-first search and strongly connected components (Chapter 6).
7. **February 3**: Breadth-first search and single-source shortest paths (Chapter 7).
8. **February 5**: Negative edge weights and all-pairs shortest paths (Chapter 8).
9. **February 10**: Boolean satisfiability, CNF satisfiability, and reduction from Boolean to CNF SAT (Chapter 9).
10. **February 12**: 2-SAT and Max 2-SAT (Chapter 10).
11. **February 24**: Subset sum (Chapter 11).
12. **February 26**: Hamiltonian path on graphs and on DAGs (Chapter 12).
13. **March 3**: Independent set, coloring, and other optimization problems on graphs and special cases of graphs (Chapter 13).
14. **March 5**: NP-Completeness and big-picture summary (Chapter 14).
15. **March 10**: Spanning trees (Chapter 16).
16. **March 12**: Disjoint paths and min-cut.
17. **March 24**: Network flow (Chapter 18).
18. **March 26**: Applications of disjoint paths and network flow (Chapter 19).
19. **April 9**: Randomized searching and sorting (Chapter 24).
20. **April 14**: The push-relabel algorithm for flow (Chapter 23).
21. **April 16**: Randomized rounding (Chapter 25).
22. **April 21**: Hashing and heavy hitters (Chapter 26).
23. **April 23**: Matchings (Chapter 21).

Please note that some topics on April 28 and April 30 are marked as TBD (to be determined).

user: Tell me about CS348

==============================
current question: Tell me about CS348

model: CS 34800, titled "Information Systems," is a course offered in Spring 2026 at Purdue University, focusing on fundamental concepts of database management, primarily using the relational model. Here are the critical details:

### Course Information
- **CRNs:** 13399 and 35118
- **Instructional Modality:** Face-to-Face
- **Course Credit Hours:** 3
- **Prerequisites:** Data Structures, Discrete Structures, and programming experience.

### Course Content
Key topics include:
- Schema design and refinement
- Query languages
- Database application development
- Transactions and concurrency control
- Overview of NoSQL databases
- Overview of OLAP and data warehousing

### Assessments and Policies
- **Homework:** Six graded assignments, must be typed and submitted in PDF format through Gradescope.
- **Quizzes:** Weekly quizzes that can be taken up to three times.
- **Exams:** Two midterm exams (open book/note).
- **Final Grade Breakdown:**
  - Assignments: 10%
  - Quizzes: 2%
  - Participation: 3%
  - Lab Exam: 20%
  - Midterm Exams: 55% (25% for 1st, 30% for 2nd)
  - Project: 10%

### Schedule & Important Dates
- Class meetings: 
  - **West Lafayette:** Mondays and Wednesdays, 5:30 PM - 6:45 PM
  - **Indianapolis:** Tuesdays and Thursdays, 10:30 AM - 11:45 AM
- **Midterm Exam 1:** March 10, 2026, 8:00 PM - 9:30 PM
- **Midterm Exam 2:** April 27, 2026, 8:00 PM - 9:30 PM
- **Project deadlines:** Stages assigned throughout the semester, with the final stage due on May 1, 2026.

Participation in class is encouraged through IClicker functionalities, contributing 3% to the total grade.

### Learning Outcomes
By the end of the course, students will be able to:
- Design effective database schemas.
- Write complex SQL queries.
- Understand transactions and data modeling.

For more specific details about class logistics, assignments, and policies, students are advised to refer to the course syllabus or the course's online platform.
